II. DEPRECIATION STUDY 
23 
Q. 
Please define the concept of depreciation. 
24 


--- Page 12 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-12- 
A. 
The FERC Uniform System of Accounts defines 
1 
depreciation as follows: 
2 
Depreciation, as applied to depreciable electric 
3 
plant, means the loss in service value not 
4 
restored by current maintenance, incurred in 
5 
connection with the consumption or prospective 
6 
retirement of electric plant in the course of 
7 
service from causes which are known to be in 
8 
current operation and against which the utility 
9 
is not protected by insurance. Among the causes 
10 
to be given consideration are wear and tear, 
11 
decay, action of the elements, inadequacy, 
12 
obsolescence, changes in the art, changes in 
13 
demand and requirements of public authorities.1 
14 
 
 
We note that the Uniform System of Accounts 
15 
specifically enumerates obsolescence, changes in 
16 
demand, requirements of public authorities and the 
17 
exhaustion of natural resources as factors that should 
18 
be given consideration.  These factors are addressed 
19 
in this testimony and will continue to be assessed by 
20 
the Company in future depreciation studies.  
21 
Q. 
In preparing the depreciation study, were generally 
22 
accepted practices in the field of depreciation 
23 
followed? 
24 
A. 
Yes. 
25 
 
1 18 C.F.R. 101 (FERC Uniform System of Accounts), Definition 12.  The 
Gas Uniform System of Accounts has a similar definition. 


--- Page 13 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-13- 
Q. 
Are the methods and procedures used in the 
1 
depreciation study consistent with the Company’s past 
2 
practices? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  The methods and procedures used in this study 
4 
are the same as those used in past depreciation 
5 
studies conducted by the Company, as well as 
6 
depreciation studies presented by other companies in 
7 
rate proceedings before the NYPSC.  The approach is to 
8 
determine depreciation rates based on the straight-
9 
line method, broad group average service life 
10 
procedure and the whole life technique.   
11 
 
Consistent with the prior depreciation study and the 
12 
Company’s current depreciation rates, we have used 
13 
Iowa type survivor curves to estimate service lives. 
 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the presentation of the depreciation 
15 
study in your exhibits. 
16 
A. 
The electric depreciation study, set forth in Exhibit 
17 
___ (DP-E1), and the gas depreciation study, set forth 
18 
in Exhibit ___ (DP-G1), are each presented in nine 
19 
parts.  Part I, Introduction, presents the scope and 
20 
basis for the depreciation study.  Parts II through V 
21 
include descriptions of the methods and procedures 
22 
used for the estimation of survivor curves and net 
23 
salvage and the calculation of annual depreciation and 
24 


--- Page 14 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-14- 
the theoretical reserve.  Part VI, Results of Study, 
1 
presents a description of the results and a summary of 
2 
the depreciation calculations.  Parts VII through IX 
3 
present graphs and tables that relate to the service 
4 
life analyses, the net salvage analyses and the 
5 
detailed depreciation calculations. 
6 
The tables on pages VI-4 through VI-6 of both Exhibit 
7 
___ (DP-E1) and Exhibit ___ (DP-G1), present the 
8 
estimated survivor curve, the net salvage percent, the 
9 
original cost of plant and the book depreciation 
10 
reserve as of December 31, 2019, and the calculated 
11 
annual depreciation accrual and applicable 
12 
depreciation rate for each plant account or 
13 
subaccount.  The section beginning on page VII-1 of 
14 
each Exhibit presents the results of the retirement 
15 
rate analyses prepared as the historical bases for the 
16 
service life estimates.  The section beginning on page 
17 
VIII-1 of each Exhibit presents the results of the 
18 
salvage analysis.  The section beginning on page IX-1 
19 
of each Exhibit presents the depreciation calculations 
20 
related to surviving original cost as of December 31, 
21 
2019.   
22 
Q. 
Please explain how the depreciation study was 
23 
performed. 
24 


--- Page 15 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-15- 
A. 
The study began with a kickoff meeting held on 
1 
September 11, 2020 between Gannett Fleming and the 
2 
Company.  Topics discussed included the overall scope 
3 
of the study, the timing of the study, the data 
4 
required to perform the depreciation study, the 
5 
logistics of conducting site visits, and factors that 
6 
may influence service lives and net salvage.  The 
7 
study was conducted over the next several months and 
8 
concluded with the reports included with our 
9 
testimony. 
10 
The study used the straight-line whole life 
11 
method of depreciation, with the broad group average 
12 
service life procedure.  The annual depreciation is 
13 
based on a method of depreciation accounting that 
14 
seeks to distribute the service value (original cost 
15 
of plant assets plus estimated costs of removal less 
16 
estimated salvage at the time of retirement) over the 
17 
estimated service life of each group of assets in a 
18 
systematic and rational manner. 
19 
Q. 
How did you determine the recommended annual 
20 
depreciation accrual rates? 
21 
A. 
This was done in two phases.  In the first phase, 
22 
estimates of the service life and net salvage factors 
23 
were developed for each depreciable group (that is, 
24 


--- Page 16 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-16- 
each plant account or subaccount identified as having 
1 
similar characteristics).  In the second phase, we 
2 
calculated the annual depreciation accrual rates using 
3 
the applicable average service lives and net salvage 
4 
factors. 
5 
Q. 
What part does the average service life play in the 
6 
determination of depreciation rates?  
7 
A. 
The estimated average service life is the period over 
8 
which the original cost of plant will be depreciated.  
9 
For example, with an average service life of 25 years, 
10 
annual depreciation is 1/25th, or 4%, of the original 
11 
cost of the plant before taking into account the net 
12 
salvage factor.   
13 
Q. 
What is the effect on annual depreciation expense of a 
14 
change to an average service life? 
15 
A. 
The depreciation expense accrual varies inversely with 
16 
its underlying average service life, and all else 
17 
being equal, the longer the average service life, the 
18 
lower the annual depreciation rate and the lower the 
19 
annual depreciation expense.  Conversely, the shorter 
20 
the average service life, the higher the annual 
21 
depreciation rate and the higher the annual 
22 
depreciation expense.   
23 


--- Page 17 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-17- 
Q. 
What part does net salvage play in the determination 
1 
of depreciation rates? 
2 
A. 
Depreciation is intended to recover the full cost of 
3 
the Company’s assets over the period of time they are 
4 
providing service.  The full cost of an asset includes 
5 
both the original cost when the asset was installed 
6 
and the net salvage at the end of the asset’s life.  
7 
Thus, in addition to providing for recovery of the 
8 
original cost of plant over its estimated average 
9 
service life, annual depreciation rates include an 
10 
estimated net salvage factor.  The purpose of this 
11 
estimated net salvage factor is to reflect, over the 
12 
life of the plant, the expected gross salvage value of 
13 
plant less the expected cost of removal upon 
14 
retirement.  With few exceptions, most plant assets 
15 
result in negative net salvage upon retirement, with 
16 
removal costs exceeding salvage value.  Salvage and 
17 
removal cost values are netted and expressed as a 
18 
percentage of original cost of plant and included in 
19 
the annual depreciation rate.  As a result, and in 
20 
accordance with basic depreciation principles and the 
21 
NYPSC’s Uniform System of Accounts, the service value 
22 
of an asset is allocated evenly over the estimated 
23 
useful life of the asset. 
24 


--- Page 18 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-18- 
Q. 
Please describe the first phase of the depreciation 
1 
study, in which you estimated the average service life 
2 
and net salvage factors for each plant account or 
3 
subaccount. 
4 
A. 
The service life and net salvage study consisted of 
5 
compiling historical data from records related to 
6 
O&R’s plant; analyzing the data to obtain historical 
7 
trends of survivor characteristics; obtaining 
8 
supplementary information from management and 
9 
operating personnel concerning practices and plans as 
10 
they relate to plant operations; making visits to 
11 
various sites to view the physical condition of 
12 
facilities; and interpreting the data and information 
13 
along with the average service lives and net salvage 
14 
factors used by other electric and gas utilities to 
15 
form judgments of average service lives and net 
16 
salvage factors applicable to O&R’s plant and 
17 
equipment. 
18 
Q. 
You mentioned that the depreciation study included 
19 
visits to O&R facilities, what is the significance of 
20 
these visits? 
21 
A. 
A field review of O&R’s property as part of the 
22 
depreciation study was made during October 2020.  
23 
Field reviews were also conducted in September 2017 
24 


--- Page 19 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-19- 
and June 2014 for the Company’s previous depreciation 
1 
studies.  Depreciation studies should not be limited 
2 
only to statistical analysis or visual comparisons of 
3 
smoothed survivor curves based on actual mortality 
4 
experience and standardized survivor curves.  Field 
5 
reviews, as well as discussions with operating and 
6 
engineering personnel, are conducted to become 
7 
familiar with Company operations and obtain an 
8 
understanding of the function of the plant and 
9 
information with respect to the reasons for past 
10 
retirements and the expected future causes of 
11 
retirements.  This knowledge, as well as information 
12 
from other discussions with management, was 
13 
incorporated in the interpretation and extrapolation 
14 
of the statistical analyses. 
15 
Q. 
What historical data was analyzed for the purpose of 
16 
estimating average service lives? 
17 
A. 
The Company’s accounting entries that record plant 
18 
asset transactions during the period 1952 through 2019 
19 
were analyzed.  The transactions included additions, 
20 
retirements, transfers and the related balances.   
21 
Q. 
What method was used to analyze the data? 
22 
A. 
The retirement rate method was used.  This is the most 
23 
appropriate method when retirement data covering a 
24 


--- Page 20 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-20- 
long period of time is available because it determines 
1 
the average rates of retirement actually experienced 
2 
by the Company during the period of time covered by 
3 
the depreciation study.  It is also the method O&R 
4 
used in past depreciation studies and is the 
5 
overwhelmingly predominant approach used in 
6 
depreciation studies across the country when aged data 
7 
is available. 
8 
Q. 
Please describe how the retirement rate method was 
9 
used to analyze the Company's service life data. 
10 
A. 
The retirement rate analysis was performed for each 
11 
different group of property, generally a particular 
12 
plant account, in the study.  For each property group, 
13 
we used the retirement rate data to form life tables 
14 
which, when plotted, show original survivor curves for 
15 
that property group.  The life tables produced for 
16 
each depreciable group include rolling and shrinking 
17 
bands, which, consistent with the Joint Proposal1 in 
18 
the Company’s previous base rate case, are included as 
19 
Exhibit ___(DP-E4) and Exhibit ___(DP-G4) to our 
20 
 
1 The Joint Proposal (p. 38) adopted by the NYPSC in Case Nos. 18-E-0067 
and 18-G-0068 states that “In the depreciation study submitted in its 
next base rate case filing, the Company will provide rolling and 
shrinking bands in the same format as it provided in these proceedings. 
These would include mathematical curve fitting results for each band. 
For rolling bands, the Company will provide rolling ten-year bands.” 


--- Page 21 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-21- 
testimony.  Each original survivor curve represents 
1 
the average survivor pattern experienced by the 
2 
vintage groups during the experience and placement 
3 
band studied.  The survivor patterns do not 
4 
necessarily describe the life characteristics of the 
5 
property group.  Therefore, interpretation of the 
6 
original survivor curves is required in order to use 
7 
them as valid considerations in estimating future 
8 
average service life.  Standard survivor curves, such 
9 
as the Iowa-type survivor curves are used to perform 
10 
these interpretations.   
11 
Q. 
What is an “Iowa-type survivor curve” and how can such 
12 
curves be used to estimate the average service life 
13 
characteristics for each property group? 
14 
A. 
Iowa-type survivor curves are a widely-used group of 
15 
survivor curves that contain the range of survivor 
16 
characteristics usually experienced by utilities and 
17 
other industrial companies.  The Iowa curves were 
18 
developed at the Iowa State College Engineering 
19 
Experiment Station through an extensive process of 
20 
observing and classifying the ages at which various 
21 
types of property used by utilities and other 
22 
industrial companies had been retired.  Iowa curves 
23 


--- Page 22 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-22- 
were used in the previous depreciation study to and 
1 
are used for Company’s current depreciation rates.  
2 
Iowa type curves are used to smooth and extrapolate 
3 
original survivor curves determined by the retirement 
4 
rate method.  The Iowa curves can be used to describe 
5 
the forecasted rates of retirement based on the 
6 
observed rates of retirement and the outlook for 
7 
future retirements. 
8 
The estimated survivor curve designations for each 
9 
depreciable property group indicate the average 
10 
service life, the family within the Iowa system to 
11 
which the property group belongs, and the relative 
12 
height of the mode.  Take the Iowa 50-R1.5, for 
13 
example.  The first designation indicates an average 
14 
service life of 50 years.  The second designation 
15 
indicates a right-moded, or R, type curve (the mode 
16 
occurs after average life for right-moded curves).  
17 
The third designation indicates a relatively low 
18 
height of 1.5, for the mode (possible modes for R type 
19 
curves range from 1 to 5). 
20

Q. 
Please describe how the proposed net salvage factors 
1 
were determined. 
2 
A. 
Consistent with well-established industry practices, 
3 
the net salvage factors were determined using informed 
4 
judgment that considered relevant factors such as the 
5 
results of historical net salvage analyses, the 
6 
existing net salvage rates in effect, the Company’s 
7 
current practices with regard to net salvage and the 
8 
net salvage factors used by other electric companies.  
9 
Q. 
Please describe the statistical net salvage analyses. 
10 
A. 
In the statistical net salvage analyses, net salvage 
11 
is expressed as a percentage of the book cost of plant 
12 
retired by calendar year.  The analysis of historical 
13 
net salvage as a percentage of the book cost of plant 
14 
retired provides a statistical basis for the level of 
15 
net salvage that can be expected to occur in the 
16 
future.   
17 
Q. 
Are the net salvage analyses and approach you used to 
18 
reflect net salvage in depreciation rates consistent 
19 
with authoritative depreciation texts? 
20 
A. 
Yes.  The National Association of Regulatory Utility 
21 
Commissioners Public Utility Depreciation Practices 
22 
(“NARUC Manual”) and Wolf and Fitch’s Depreciation 
23 
Systems (“Wolf and Fitch”) are well-regarded texts 
24 


--- Page 26 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-26- 
that are considered to be authoritative depreciation 
1 
sources by depreciation professionals.  These texts 
2 
describe the method of estimating net salvage and 
3 
explain that expected net salvage at the time of 
4 
retirement of plant assets is expressed as a 
5 
percentage of original cost of the plant that will be 
6 
retired and is estimated using the same methods we 
7 
have employed.   
8 
Q. 
Are the methods used in the depreciation study for the 
9 
net salvage analysis widely accepted in the industry? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  The net salvage analysis used in the Company’s 
11 
depreciation study is the predominant approach in the 
12 
utility industry.  In the vast majority of 
13 
jurisdictions, including New York, a portion of 
14 
depreciation expense includes a provision for the 
15 
prospective recovery of future net salvage over the 
16 
service life of the underlying assets, and the net 
17 
salvage factors are estimated using the same methods 
18 
used in the Company’s depreciation study.  This 
19 
approach is consistent with the NYPSC Uniform System 
20 
of Accounts, the ratemaking practices of most other 
21 
state regulatory commissions, and the ratemaking 
22 
approach of the FERC. 
23

-28- 
Q. 
In addition to the pipe replacement program, are there 
1 
any other notable changes to the service life 
2 
estimates for gas assets? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  We propose in this case to shorten the service 
4 
lives for the longer-lived gas accounts (i.e., 
5 
structures and improvements, mains, services, meter 
6 
installations, meter bar installations and house 
7 
regulator installations) by five years from what the 
8 
Company has historically experienced.   
9 
Q. 
Why is the Company proposing this change? 
10 
A. 
O&R is fully aware of, and supports, the state of New 
11 
York’s goals to reduce greenhouse gas emissions to net 
12 
zero by the year 2050.  The State is still developing 
13 
CLCPA regulations, so the pathway to achieve such 
14 
carbon emissions reductions is unknown, and it is 
15 
likely to change even after the State finalizes and 
16 
adopts its regulations.  It may be, however, that the 
17 
State will seek to achieve decarbonization with 
18 
heating electrification as a component.   O&R is 
19 
supporting the overall effort through its 
20 
Electrification of Heating - Heat Pump Demonstration 
21 
Project.  It may also be, however, that the State 
22 
achieves decarbonization through a balance of relying 
23 
on support of renewable generation for heating 
24 


--- Page 29 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-29- 
electrification, as well as efforts to decarbonize the 
1 
fuels delivered using our existing gas infrastructure.  
2 
The differences in pathways are significant in terms 
3 
of the impact on the gas system and it is too early to 
4 
determine which is most appropriate.  The eventual 
5 
pathway will depend on specific CLCPA regulations, as 
6 
well as new and next generation technologies.1  
7 
Regardless of the pathway, in order to begin alignment 
8 
with the CLCPA’s goals, the Company believes that it 
9 
makes sense to begin planning for a potential 
10 
reduction in total delivered gas volume.  Accordingly, 
11 
the Company has included a reduction to certain gas 
12 
average service lives in this case.  This is 
13 
appropriate because changes in energy consumption 
14 
would impact the Company’s service lives.  For 
15 
example, if a customer leaves the system, there would 
16 
be retirements of the meter, service line and any 
17 
other infrastructure at the customer locations.  If 
18 
enough customers were to leave the system, this could 
19 
also impact other assets such as gas mains and 
20 
measuring and regulating station equipment.   
21 
 
1 For example, technologies requiring breakthroughs for low carbon fuels 
– these include zero carbon such as green hydrogen, carbon neutral such 
as green methane, and carbon negative such as renewable natural gas. 


--- Page 30 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-30- 
To be clear, there is substantial uncertainty on the 
1 
timing and pathway for CLCPA compliance and major 
2 
changes in gas depreciation are not currently 
3 
warranted.  Given the CLCPA’s ambitious goals, 
4 
however, the Company believes a modest change in 
5 
service lives is both appropriate and advisable.   As 
6 
the pathways to achieving the CLCPA’s goals further 
7 
develop, the Company will assess their impact on the 
8 
depreciable lives of its gas assets.  This stepped 
9 
approach recognizes the impact of the State’s goals 
10 
and the reality that the pathways to reach it are 
11 
likely to affect the gas business, while maintaining 
12 
flexibility in approach.  This approach will allow the 
13 
Company to adapt to future trends, regulations and 
14 
technological advances. Finally, the Company notes 
15 
that if it turns out that gas heating will be 
16 
eliminated at some point in the future as part of 
17 
CLCPA goal achievement, then this is an important 
18 
first step that the Company can continue and/or 
19 
accelerate in future gas rate cases.

Q. 
Do you have a recommendation regarding the reserve 
10 
variations? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  For electric and common plant, we recommend no 
12 
action be taken related to the reserve variations, at 
13 
the levels indicated, at this time.  The NYPSC’s 
14 
customary practice has been to take no remedial action 
15 
when the book reserve varies from the theoretical 
16 
reserve by less than 10% (plus or minus).  The 
17 
variations for electric and common plant fall within 
18 
that range. 
19 
 
For gas plant, we recommend that the full reserve 
20 
variation amount be recovered over a 20-year period.  
21 
The estimated reserve variation for gas plant differs 
22 
from the theoretical reserve by more than 10%, which 
23 
indicates a need for remedial action.  However, even 
24 


--- Page 34 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-34- 
if the reserve variation did not exceed this 10% 
1 
threshold, this recovery period would be appropriate 
2 
in light of the factors discussed in the previous 
3 
section related to New York’s greenhouse gas emissions 
4 
goals.  Absent any action to address the reserve 
5 
variation, if decarbonization does result in the loss 
6 
of customers and shorter service lives, then the 
7 
Company would not have the opportunity to recover the 
8 
full costs of its assets over their service lives.  
9 
Even if cost recovery were deferred, the result would 
10 
be future customers paying for assets that are already 
11 
retired, which would result in intergenerational 
12 
inequity. 
13 
In addition, the issues related to decarbonization 
14 
argue for the amortization of the full reserve 
15 
variation, rather than a portion of this amount.  In 
16 
prior cases, there have been proposals to recover only 
17 
the amount in excess of 10% of the theoretical 
18 
reserve.  Given the issues facing the natural gas 
19 
industry, failing to recover the full reserve 
20 
variation could mean that the Company will be denied 
21 
the opportunity to recover the full costs of its 
22 
assets. 
23 
 
For these reasons, it is appropriate to recover the 
24 


--- Page 35 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-35- 
full reserve variation for gas plant that results from 
1 
the depreciation study.  We recommend a 20-year 
2 
recovery of these costs, which is a period that has 
3 
been used in previous studies.

IV. ADVANCED METERING INFRASTRUCTURE 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please discuss the Company’s recovery of its 
7 
investment in “legacy” meters due to the 
8 
implementation of its AMI program. 
9 
A. 
AMI is a technology for improving efficiencies related 
10 
to meter reading and providing other system and 
11 
customer benefits.  These initiatives involve 
12 
installing electric “smart meters” across O&R’s 
13 
service territory, resulting in the phasing-out of the 
14 
older, “legacy” technology (i.e., electro-mechanical 
15 
and solid state meters) before they are fully 
16 
depreciated. The Company began deploying AMI meters in 
17 
2017 is completed the initiative in 2020. 
18 
  
 
In the Joint Proposal (p. 38) in Case No. 18-E-
19 
0067, the parties agreed that “[b]eginning in RY1, the 
20 
Company will amortize unrecovered legacy meter costs 
21 
due to the implementation of AMI. Once AMI is fully 
22 
deployed, the Company will defer as a separate 
23 
regulatory asset the remaining undepreciated 
24 


--- Page 36 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
DEPRECIATION PANEL 
 
-36- 
investment in legacy meters and recover it over a 15-
1 
year period.” 
2 
Q.   What is the Company’s proposal regarding the recovery 
3 
of the remaining book cost for electric meters that 
4 
have been and will be retired due to the 
5 
implementation of AMI? 
6 
A.   The Company proposes to continue the recovery 
7 
established in Case No. 18-E-0067, which is discussed 
8 
in more detail in the direct testimony of the 
9 
Company’s Accounting Panel.  Additionally, it is our 
10 
understanding that a relatively small number of meters 
11 
will remain in service at the completion of the AMI 
12 
program.  The recommended depreciation rate for the 
13 
legacy meters that will remain is based on a 20-year 
14 
average service life, which is the same average 
15 
service life that had been used for solid state meters 
16 
and is currently used for AMI meters. 
17 
Q. 
Does this conclude your direct testimony?

ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DEPRECIATION STUDY 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
Pursuant to the request of Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. (“O&R” or 
“Company”), Gannett Fleming Valuation and Rate Consultants, LLC (“Gannett Fleming”) 
conducted a depreciation study related to O&R’s gas and common plant as of 
December 31, 2019. The purpose of this study was to determine the annual 
depreciation accrual rates and amounts for book and ratemaking purposes. 
The depreciation rates are based on the straight-line method using the broad 
group average service life (“ASL”) procedure and were applied on a whole life basis. 
The calculations were based on attained ages and estimated average service life and 
net salvage for each depreciable group of assets. 
Changes in service life and net salvage estimates have not been significant for 
many accounts. Relatively gradual changes are recommended for most accounts as 
compared to the previous depreciation study as of December 31, 2016.  For gas plant, 
somewhat shorter service lives and slightly more negative net salvage estimates are 
recommended for some distribution accounts.  Shorter service lives are due in part to 
consideration of the potential impacts of New York State goals for greenhouse gas 
emissions.  In total, the proposed changes for gas plant, including the amortization of 
the calculated reserve deficiency, result in an increase in annual depreciation expense. 
For common plant, longer service lives are recommended for transportation equipment 
accounts which results in a decrease to annual depreciation expense for common plant.  
Gannett Fleming recommends the calculated annual depreciation accrual rates 
set forth herein apply specifically to gas and common plant in service as of December

December 31, 2019

are provided within the study. 
The study results set forth an annual depreciation expense of $26.5 million when 
applied to depreciable plant balances of gas and common plant as of December 31, 
2019. The results of the study, including the reserve variations, are summarized at the 
functional level as follows:

ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DEPRECIATION STUDY 
 
 
PART I. INTRODUCTION 
 
SCOPE 
 
This report sets forth the results of the depreciation study for Orange and 
Rockland Utilities, Inc. (“O&R”), to determine the annual depreciation accrual rates and 
amounts for book purposes applicable to the original cost of gas and common plant as 
of December 31, 2019. The rates and amounts are based on the straight line whole life 
method of depreciation. This report also describes the concepts, methods and 
judgments which underlie the recommended annual depreciation accrual rates related 
to gas and common plant in service as of December 31, 2019. 
The service life and net salvage estimates resulting from the study were based 
on informed judgment which incorporated analyses of historical plant retirement data as 
recorded through 2019, a review of Company practice and outlook as they relate to 
plant operation and retirement, and consideration of current practice in the gas industry, 
including knowledge of service lives and net salvage estimates used for other gas 
companies. 
PLAN OF REPORT 
 
Part I, Introduction, contains statements with respect to the plan of the report, 
and the basis of the study. Part II, Estimation of Survivor Curves, presents descriptions 
of the considerations and the methods used in the service life and net salvage studies. 
Part III, Service Life Considerations, presents the factors and judgment utilized in the 
average service life analysis.  Part IV, Net Salvage Considerations, presents the
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
I-2
Exhibit___DP-G1
9 of 192


--- Page 46 ---
 
judgment utilized for the net salvage study. Part V, Calculation of Annual and Accrued 
Depreciation, describes the procedures used in the calculation of group depreciation. 
Part VI, Results of Study, presents summaries by depreciable group of annual 
depreciation accrual rates and amounts, as well as the calculated accrued depreciation, 
reserve variations and composite remaining lives. Part VII, Service Life Statistics 
presents the statistical analysis of service life estimates, Part VIII, Net Salvage Statistics 
sets forth the statistical indications of net salvage percents, and Part IX, Detailed 
Depreciation Calculations presents the detailed tabulations of annual and accrued 
depreciation. 
BASIS OF THE STUDY 
 
Depreciation 
 
Depreciation, in public utility regulation, is the loss in service value not restored 
by current maintenance, incurred in connection with the consumption or prospective 
retirement of utility plant in the course of service from causes which are known to be in 
current operation and against which the utility is not protected by insurance. Among 
causes to be given consideration are wear and tear, deterioration, action of the 
elements, inadequacy, obsolescence, changes in the art, changes in demand, and the 
requirements of public authorities. 
Depreciation, as used in accounting, is a method of distributing fixed capital 
costs, less net salvage, over a period of time by allocating annual amounts to expense. 
Each annual amount of such depreciation expense is part of that year's total cost of 
providing utility service. Normally, the period of time over which the fixed capital cost is 
allocated to the cost of service is equal to the period of time over which an item renders 
service, that is, the item's service life.  The most prevalent method of allocation is to 
distribute an equal amount of cost to each year of service life. This method is known as 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
I-3
Exhibit___DP-G1
10 of 192


--- Page 47 ---
 
the straight-line method of depreciation. 
For most accounts, the annual depreciation was calculated by the straight-line 
method using the average service life procedure and the whole life basis. For certain 
General Plant accounts, the annual depreciation is based on amortization accounting. 
Both types of calculations were based on original cost, attained ages, and estimates of 
service lives and net salvage. 
The straight-line method, average service life procedure is a commonly used 
depreciation calculation procedure that has been widely accepted in jurisdictions 
throughout North America, including New York. Gannett Fleming recommends its use in 
this study. Amortization accounting is used for certain General Plant accounts because 
of the disproportionate plant accounting effort required when compared to the minimal 
original cost of the large number of items in these accounts. An explanation of the 
calculation of annual and accrued amortization is presented beginning on page V-3 of 
the report. 
 
Service Life and Net Salvage Estimates 
 
The service life and net salvage estimates used in the depreciation and 
amortization calculations were based on informed judgment which incorporated a review 
of management’s plans, policies and outlook, a general knowledge of the gas utility 
industry, and comparisons of the service life and net salvage estimates from our studies 
of other gas utilities. The use of survivor curves to reflect the expected dispersion of 
retirement provides a consistent method of estimating depreciation for utility plant. Iowa 
type survivor curves were used to depict the estimated survivor curves for the plant 
accounts not subject to amortization accounting. 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
I-4
Exhibit___DP-G1
11 of 192


--- Page 48 ---
The procedure for estimating service lives consisted of compiling historical data 
for the plant accounts or depreciable groups, analyzing this history through the use of 
widely accepted techniques, and forecasting the survivor characteristics for each 
depreciable group on the basis of interpretations of the historical data analyses and the 
probable future. The combination of the historical experience and the estimated future 
yielded estimated survivor curves from which the average service lives were derived.

PART II.  ESTIMATION OF SURVIVOR CURVES 
 
The calculation of annual depreciation based on the straight-line method requires 
the estimation of survivor curves and the selection of group depreciation procedures. 
The estimation of survivor curves is discussed in the following section and the 
development of net salvage is discussed in later sections of this report. 
 
SURVIVOR CURVES 
 
The use of an average service life for a property group implies that the various 
units in the group have different lives. Thus, the average life may be obtained by 
determining the separate lives of each of the units or by constructing a survivor curve by 
plotting the number of units which survive at successive ages. 
The survivor curve graphically depicts the amount of property existing at each 
age throughout the life of an original group. From the survivor curve, the average life of 
the group, the remaining life expectancy, the probable life, and the frequency curve can 
be calculated. In Figure 1, a typical smooth survivor curve and the derived curves are 
illustrated. The average life is obtained by calculating the area under the survivor curve, 
from age zero to the maximum age, and dividing this area by the ordinate at age zero. 
The remaining life expectancy at any age can be calculated by obtaining the area under 
the curve, from the observation age to the maximum age, and dividing this area by the 
percent surviving at the observation age. For example, in Figure 1, the remaining life at 
age 30 is equal to the crosshatched area under the survivor curve divided by 29.5 
percent surviving at age 30. The probable life at any age is developed by adding the 
age and remaining life. If the probable life of the property is calculated for each year of 
age, the probable life curve shown in the chart can be developed. The frequency curve 
presents the number of units retired in each age interval. It is derived by obtaining the 
differences between the amount of property surviving at the beginning and at the end of 
each interval. 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
II-2
Exhibit___DP-G1
14 of 192


--- Page 51 ---
 
The recommended survivor curves in this study are developed from a retirement 
rate analysis of historical retirement data and are based on Iowa type survivor curves. A 
discussion of the concepts of survivor curves and of the development of survivor curves 
using the retirement rate method is presented below. 
Iowa Type Curves 
The range of survivor characteristics usually experienced by utility and industrial 
properties is encompassed by a system of generalized survivor curves known as the 
Iowa type curves.  There are four families in the Iowa system, labeled in accordance 
with the location of the modes of the retirements in relationship to the average life and 
the relative height of the modes. The left moded curves, presented in Figure 2, are 
those in which the greatest frequency of retirement occurs to the left of, or prior to, 
average service life.  The symmetrical moded curves, presented in Figure 3, are those 
in which the greatest frequency of retirement occurs at average service life. The right 
moded curves, presented in Figure 4, are those in which the greatest frequency occurs 
to the right of, or after, average service life. The origin moded curves, presented in 
Figure 5, are those in which the greatest frequency of retirement occurs at the origin, or 
immediately after age zero.     The letter designation of each family of curves (L, S, R or 
O) represents the location of the mode of the associated frequency curve with respect 
to the average service life. The numbers represent the relative heights of the modes of 
the frequency curves within each family. 
The Iowa curves were developed at the Iowa State College Engineering 
Experiment Station through an extensive process of observation and classification of 
the ages at which industrial property had been retired. A report of the study which 
resulted in the classification of property survivor characteristics into 18 type curves, 
which constitute three of the four families, was published in 1935 in the form of the 
Experiment Station’s Bulletin 125.

These curve types have also been presented in subsequent Experiment Station 
bulletins and in the text, "Engineering Valuation and Depreciation."1 In 1957, Frank V. B. 
Couch, Jr., an Iowa State College graduate student submitted a thesis presenting his 
development of the fourth family consisting of the four O type survivor curves. 
Retirement Rate Method of Analysis 
 
The retirement rate method is an actuarial method of deriving survivor curves 
using the average rates at which property of each age group is retired.  The method 
relates to property groups for which aged accounting experience is available and is the 
method used to develop the original stub survivor curves in this study.  The method (also 
known as the annual rate method) is illustrated through the use of an example in the 
following text and is also explained in several publications, including "Statistical Analyses 
of Industrial Property Retirements”, 2 "Engineering Valuation and Depreciation," 3 and 
"Depreciation Systems."4 
The average rate of retirement used in the calculation of the percent surviving for 
the survivor curve (life table) requires two sets of data: first, the property retired during a 
period of observation, identified by the property's age at retirement; and second, the 
property exposed to retirement at the beginning of the age intervals during the same 
period.  The period of observation is referred to as the experience band, and the band of 
years which represent the installation dates of the property exposed to retirement during 
the experience band is referred to as the placement band.  An example of the 
calculations used in the development of a life table follows. The example includes 
 
1 Marston, Anson, Robley Winfrey and Jean C. Hempstead.  Engineering Valuation and Depreciation, 
2nd Edition.  New York, McGraw-Hill Book Company. 1953. 
2 Winfrey, Robley, Statistical Analyses of Industrial Property Retirements.  Iowa State College 
Engineering Experiment Station, Bulletin 125.  1935. 
3Marston, Anson, Robley Winfrey, and Jean C. Hempstead, Supra Note 1. 
4Wolf, Frank K. and W. Chester Fitch. Depreciation Systems.  Iowa State University Press.  1994.

schedules of annual aged property transactions, a schedule of plant exposed to 
retirement, a life table and illustrations of smoothing the stub survivor curve. 
Schedules of Annual Transactions in Plant Records 
The property group used to illustrate the retirement rate method is observed for the 
experience band 2010-2019 during which there were placements in years 2005-2019.  To 
illustrate the summation of the aged data by age interval, the data were compiled in the 
manner presented in Schedules 1 and 2 on pages II-12 and II-13.  In Schedule 1, the year 
of installation (year placed) and the year of retirement are shown. The age interval during 
which a retirement occurred is determined from this information. In the example which 
follows, $10,000 of the dollars invested in 2005 were retired in 2010. The $10,000 
retirement occurred during the age interval between 4½ and 5½ years on the basis that 
approximately one-half of the amount of property was installed prior to and subsequent to 
July 1 of each year. That is, on average, property installed during a year is placed in 
service at the midpoint of the year for the purpose of the analysis. All retirements also are 
stated as occurring at the midpoint of a one-year age interval of time, except the first age 
interval which encompasses only one- half year. 
The total retirements occurring in each age interval in a band are determined by 
summing the amounts for each transaction year-installation year combination for that age 
interval. For example, the total of $143,000 retired for age interval 4½-5½ is the sum of 
the retirements entered on Schedule 1 immediately above the stair step line drawn on the 
table beginning with the 2010 retirements of 2005 installations and ending with the 2019 
retirements of the 2014 installations. Thus, the total amount of 143 for age interval 4½-
5½ equals the sum of: 
10 + 12 + 13 + 11 + 13 + 13 + 15 + 17 + 19 + 20. 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
II-10
Exhibit___DP-G1
22 of 192


--- Page 59 ---
In Schedule 2, other transactions which affect the group are recorded in a similar 
manner. The entries illustrated include transfers and sales. The entries which are 
credits to the plant account are shown in parentheses.  The items recorded on this  
schedule are not totaled with the retirements but are used in developing the exposures 
at the beginning of each age interval. 
Schedule of Plant Exposed to Retirement 
The development of the amount of plant exposed to retirement at the beginning 
of each age interval is illustrated in Schedule 3 on page II-14. The surviving plant at the 
beginning of each year from 2010 through 2019 is recorded by year in the portion of the 
table headed "Annual Survivors at the Beginning of the Year." The last amount entered 
in each column is the amount of new plant added to the group during the year. The 
amounts entered in Schedule 3 for each successive year following the beginning 
balance or addition are obtained by adding or subtracting the net entries shown on 
Schedules 1 and 2.  For the purpose of determining the plant exposed to retirement, 
transfers-in are considered as being exposed to retirement in this group at the 
beginning of the year in which they occurred, and the sales and transfers-out are 
considered to be removed from the plant exposed to retirement at the beginning of the 
following year.  Thus, the amounts of plant shown at the beginning of each year are the 
amounts of plant from each placement year considered to be exposed to retirement at 
the beginning of each successive transaction year. For example, the exposures for the 
installation year 2015 are calculated in the following manner: 
Exposures at age 0    = amount of addition 
= $750,000 
Exposures at age ½   = $750,000 - $ 8,000 
= $742,000 
Exposures at age 1½ = $742,000 - $18,000 
= $724,000 
Exposures at age 2½ = $724,000 - $20,000 - $19,000 = $685,000 
Exposures at age 3½ = $685,000 - $22,000 
= $663,000

For the entire experience band 2010-2019, the total exposures at the beginning 
of an age interval are obtained by summing diagonally in a manner similar to the 
summing of the retirements during an age interval (Table 1). For example, the figure of 
3,789, shown as the total exposures at the beginning of age interval 4½-5½, is obtained 
by summing: 
255 + 268 + 284 + 311 + 334 + 374 + 405 + 448 + 501 + 609. 
 
Original Life Table 
 
The original life table, illustrated in Schedule 4 on page II-16, is developed from 
the totals shown on the schedules of retirements and exposures, Schedules 1 and 3, 
respectively.  The exposures at the beginning of the age interval are obtained from the 
corresponding age interval of the exposure schedule, and the retirements during the 
age interval are obtained from the corresponding age interval of the retirement 
schedule. 
The retirement ratio is the result of dividing the retirements during the age 
interval by the exposures at the beginning of the age interval. The percent surviving at 
the beginning of each age interval is derived from survivor ratios, each of which equals 
one minus the retirement ratio.  The percent surviving is developed by starting with 
100% at age zero and successively multiplying the percent surviving at the beginning of 
each interval by the survivor ratio, i.e., one minus the retirement ratio for that age 
interval.  The calculations necessary to determine the percent surviving at age 5½ are 
as follows: 
Percent surviving at age 4½ 
= 
88.15 
Exposures at age 4½ 
= 3,789,000 
Retirements from age 4½ to 5½ = 
143,000 
Retirement Ratio 
= 
143,000 ÷ 3,789,000 = 0.0377 
Survivor Ratio 
= 
 1.000 - 
 0.0377 = 0.9623 
Percent surviving at age 5½ 
= 
(88.15) x 
(0.9623)  = 84.83

The totals of the exposures and retirements (columns 2 and 3) are shown for the 
purpose of checking with the respective totals in Schedules 1 and 3. The ratio of the 
total retirements to the total exposures, other than for each age interval, is meaningless. 
The original survivor curve is plotted from the original life table (column 6, Schedule 4). 
When the curve terminates at a percent surviving greater than zero, it is called a stub 
survivor curve. Survivor curves developed from retirement rate studies generally are 
stub curves. 
Smoothing the Original Survivor Curve 
 
The smoothing of the original survivor curve eliminates any irregularities and 
serves as the basis for the preliminary extrapolation to zero percent surviving of the 
original stub curve. Even if the original survivor curve is complete from 100% to zero 
percent, it is desirable to eliminate any irregularities, as there is still an extrapolation for 
the vintages which have not yet lived to the age at which the curve reaches zero 
percent. In this study, the smoothing of the original curve with established type curves 
was used to eliminate irregularities in the original curve. 
The Iowa type curves are used in this study to smooth those original stub curves 
which are expressed as percents surviving at ages in years. Each original survivor 
curve was compared to the Iowa type curves using visual and mathematical matching to 
determine the better fitting smooth curves. In Figures 7, 8, and 9, the original curve 
developed in Table 4 is compared with the L, S, and R Iowa type curves which most 
nearly fit the original survivor curve.  In Figure 7, the L1 curve with an average life 
between 12 and 13 years appears to be the best fit. In Figure 8, the S0 type curve with 
a 12-year average life appears to be the best fit and appears to be better fitting than the 
L1.  In Figure 9, the R1 type curve with a 12- year average life appears to be the best fit 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
II-17
Exhibit___DP-G1
29 of 192


--- Page 66 ---
5 
and appears to be better than either the L1 or the S0.   
In Figure 10, the three fittings, 12-L1, 12-S0 and 12-R1 are drawn for comparison 
purposes. It is probable that the 12-R1 Iowa curve would be selected as the most 
representative of the plotted survivor characteristics of the group.

PART III.  SERVICE LIFE CONSIDERATIONS 
 
 
FIELD TRIPS 
 
In order to be familiar with the operation of the Company and observe 
representative portions of the plant, a field trip was conducted for the study. A general 
understanding of the function of the plant and information with respect to the reasons for 
past retirements and the expected future causes of retirements are obtained during field 
trips. This knowledge and information were incorporated in the interpretation and 
extrapolation of the statistical analyses. 
The following is a list of the locations visited during the most recent field trips. 
 
October 1, 2020 
Suffern City Gate Station 
Suffern District Station 
September 6, 2017 
Suffern City Gate Station 
Suffern District Station 
June 24, 2014 
Buena Vista City Gate Station 
South Mountain Road Measuring and Regulating Station 
Main Replacement Project, Theis Lane, Blauvelt, NY 
 
SERVICE LIFE ANALYSIS 
 
The service life estimates were based on informed judgment which considered a 
number of factors. The primary factors were the statistical analyses of data; current 
Company policies and outlook as determined during conversations with management; 
and the survivor curve estimates from previous studies of this company and other gas 
companies. 
For many of the plant accounts and subaccounts for which survivor curves were 
estimated, the statistical analyses using the retirement rate method resulted in 
reasonable indications of the survivor patterns experienced. These accounts represent 
68 percent of depreciable plant. Generally, the information external to the statistics led

Account 378, Measuring and Regulating Station Equipment, is used to provide an 
example of how many of the service life estimates were determined for the assets in the 
above list.  Aged retirement and other plant accounting data were compiled for the years 
1952 through 2019. These data were coded in the course of the Company’s normal 
recordkeeping according to plant account or property group, type of transaction, year in 
which the transaction took place, and year in which the gas plant was placed in service.  
The data were analyzed by the retirement rate method of life analysis. The survivor curve 
chart for the account is presented on page VII-16 and the life tables for the experience 
band plotted on the chart follows it. 
The current estimate for this account is the 35-L0.5.  The life analysis for the 
current study indicate that a lower mode S-curve would be a better fit of the most 
representative portion of the observed life table.  To continue to use an L-curve would

mean increasing the service life, which would lead to maximum lives resulting from an L-
type curve that would not be reasonable for the assets in this account.  The 35-S0 is a 
good fit of the historical data and has the same average service life as the current 
estimate.  The 35-S0 is the recommended survivor curve estimate for this account. 
The largest plant accounts are those for gas mains and gas services.  There were 
a number of factors considered for the service life estimates for these accounts.  In 
addition to the statistical analysis, consideration was also given to the Company’s 
program to replace aging pipe over the next ten years as well as New York State 
decarbonization goals that could impact the natural gas industry in the state. 
Accounts 367 and 376, which are the transmission and distribution mains 
accounts, were studied together.  For these accounts, aged retirement and other plant 
accounting data were compiled for the years 1952 through 2019. The data were analyzed 
by the retirement rate method of life analysis. The survivor curve chart for the account is 
presented on page VII-11 and the life tables for the experience band plotted on the chart 
follows it. Data were available to develop depreciation rates by material type.  The 
Company plans to continue its replacement of existing leak-prone cast iron, bare steel 
and some late 1960’s and 1970’s vintage plastic mains in the coming years.  The 
company plans to replace all of these leak-prone pipe assets by the year 2029.  Separate 
subaccounts for each of these types of mains have been established with depreciation 
rates and accruals developed based on the retirement of these assets by the end of 
2029. 
For the remaining gas mains, the statistical life analysis indicated survivor curves 
with average service lives in the 65 to 70 year range.  In the previous study, the 70-S1.5 
was recommended and the 71-S1.5 was adopted in a settlement agreement.  The 65-R3 
survivor curve is a good fit of the historical data and incorporates retirement rates for 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
III-4
Exhibit___DP-G1
39 of 192


--- Page 76 ---
 
older mains that are more consistent with expectations for these assets than survivor 
curves with average service lives of 70 years or longer.  These expectations are based in 
part on the fact that the state of New York has goals to significantly reduce carbon 
emissions by 2050.  While the precise path to attain these goals is unknown at this time, 
it is reasonable to expect that these goals could impact natural gas consumption and, in 
turn, the service lives of assets that provide natural gas to customers.  The target date for 
these significant greenhouse gas reductions is approximately 30 years from now.  Many 
of the service lives of accounts in the depreciation study, such as gas services and 
mains, have historically had lives that far exceed this period of 30 years.  As a result, the 
impact of decarbonization could be that the economic lives of the Company’s assets will 
be significantly shorter than has occurred historically.  These factors were considered 
when estimating service lives in the depreciation study, and in particular for gas services, 
gas mains, and other assets with longer service lives.  In many instances, the service life 
estimates for these accounts are good matches to the earlier portions of the original life 
table and focus on fitting a survivor curve to this portion of the data due to the uncertainty 
as to whether assets will remain in service long enough to reach older ages of the original 
life table. Based on these considerations, the 65-R3 estimate is most representative of 
future experience for gas mains.  This estimate is a good fit of a large portion of the 
historical data but also incorporates future expectations and risks for the gas industry due 
to the potential impact of decarbonization goals. 
The second largest account, Account 380, Services, was not included on the 
above list because of the potential impact of factors such as New York’s decarbonization 
goals and the Company’s replacement of leak-prone pipe.  However, this does not mean 
that the statistical analysis of historical data was not considered.  Similar to other 
accounts, for Account 380, Services, the historical data were analyzed by the retirement 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
III-5
Exhibit___DP-G1
40 of 192


--- Page 77 ---
 
rate method of life analysis.  The survivor curve for the account is presented on page VII-
20 and the life tables for the 1952-2019 and 2010-2019 experience bands are presented 
on the pages that follow the survivor curve.  The current estimate for the services account 
is the 65-R3.  The statistical analysis of the data indicates that the 65-R3 is still a 
reasonable fit to the observed life table.  However, as a result of the factors discussed 
above, it is expected that the service life characteristics in the future will be different from 
those in the past.  Similar to the mains account, there is leak-prone plastic pipe in the 
system with mid-1960’s to mid-1970’s vintages and leak-prone steel pipe that is expected 
to be replaced by 2029.  Accordingly, this account was also subdivided into subaccounts 
by material type and depreciation rates developed for leak-prone pipe based on the 
expected end of the program in 2029.  Additionally, New York’s goals for decarbonization 
were considered for this account.  This account could be particularly affected by these 
goals, because if customers change their energy consumption from natural gas to 
another energy source, they will no longer need a gas service line and the service line 
would be retired.  As a result, it is reasonable to expect that service lives for this account 
will be shorter in the future than in the past.   
For the overall band for Account 380, Services, the 60-R3 is a good fit of the 
earlier portion of the data, follows the overall shape of the original life table curve and 
also reaches 0% surviving at a very similar age to the original life table curve.  The more 
recent experience band also provides some support for a shorter life, as that original life 
table for the 2010 to 2019 experience band declines more rapidly past age 60 than the 
overall experience band, and approaches 0% surviving at a younger age than the overall 
experience band.  For all of these reasons, the 60-R3 is the most appropriate estimate for 
this account.

lives are expected in the future than have occurred historically.  As a result, the service 
life estimates for each of these accounts are shorter than those in the previous 
depreciation study.

PART IV.  NET SALVAGE CONSIDERATIONS 
 
NET SALVAGE ANALYSIS 
 
The estimates of net salvage by account were based in part on historical data 
compiled for the years 1983 through 2019. Cost of removal and gross salvage were 
expressed as percents of the original cost of plant retired, both on annual and three- 
year moving average bases. The most recent five-year average also was calculated for 
consideration. The net salvage estimates by account are expressed as a percent of the 
original cost of plant retired. 
Net Salvage Considerations 
 
The estimates of future net salvage are expressed as percentages of surviving 
plant in service, i.e., all future retirements. In cases in which removal costs are expected 
to exceed gross salvage receipts, a negative net salvage percentage is estimated. The 
net salvage estimates were based on judgment which incorporated analyses of 
historical cost of removal and gross salvage data, expectations with respect to future 
removal requirements and markets for retired equipment and materials. 
The analyses of historical cost of removal and gross salvage data are presented 
in the section titled “Net Salvage Statistics” for the plant accounts for which the net 
salvage estimate is based in part on the net salvage analysis.  Transactions were 
reviewed in this study and previous studied to determine whether there was any activity 
such as sales or abnormal retirements that should be excluded from the net salvage 
analysis.  No such activity was identified for gas or general plant assets. 
Statistical analyses of historical data for the period 1983 through 2019 
contributed toward the net salvage estimates for the plant accounts listed below, which 
represent 96 percent of the depreciable plant. For many of these accounts, the net 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
IV-2
Exhibit___DP-G1
44 of 192


--- Page 81 ---
 
salvage estimates recommended are conservative (i.e. less negative) when compared 
to the historical data, and particularly the recent historical data.

GAS PLANT

375

Two of the largest property groups are Accounts 367 and 376, Mains, which are 
studied together. These accounts will be used to illustrate the methods for estimating 
net salvage.  The net salvage estimate for these accounts is negative 40 percent, 
which is based on factors including the historical net salvage analyses as shown in the 
tabulation on pages VIII-5 and VIII-6; the existing net salvage estimate; and the typical 
range of net salvage estimates used by other gas utilities for Accounts 367 and 376, 
Mains.  The historical indication for the period 1983 through 2019 is negative 50 
percent. The existing estimate for this account is negative 30 percent, although this 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
IV-3
Exhibit___DP-G1
45 of 192


--- Page 82 ---
 
was based on a settlement agreement.  The net salvage estimate in the previous study 
was negative 50 percent, which is more consistent with the overall data. 
When developing a net salvage estimate it is helpful to understand the likely 
disposition of the assets upon retirement. The major types of asset groups that 
comprise this account include plastic and steel mains.  The retirement of gas mains 
typically requires excavation, cutting, purging gas and capping the retired main.  O&R 
has experienced removal costs in the past and it is reasonable to expect the company 
will continue to incur removal costs related to gas mains in the future.  The proposed 
net salvage estimate of negative 40 percent is supported by company experience 
during the past 37 years.  However, a less negative net salvage estimate is 
recommended than the estimate from the previous study in part because more recent 
data has indicated less negative net salvage.  The recommended negative 40 percent 
net salvage estimate is also within the typical range of net salvage estimates used by 
other gas companies and consistent with Company expectations.    
The second largest plant account is Account 380, Services.  The historical net 
salvage analysis for this account is located on pages VIII-9 and VIII-10 of the 
depreciation study.  The analysis was conducted on 37 years of historical net salvage 
data from 1983 through 2019.  The current estimate for this account is negative 80 
percent, which at the time of the last study was a conservative estimate when 
compared to the historical data.  A negative 90 percent net salvage estimate is 
recommended in this study for this account.  This estimate reflects the indications from 
the analysis of historical net salvage data, although it is a conservative estimate when 
compared to the data.  The overall average net salvage in the data is negative 236 
percent.  Additionally, the three-year averages have been steadily increasing since the

Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc.

IV-4
Exhibit___DP-G1

--- Page 83 ---
 
early 2000s and the most recent five-year average is negative 467 percent.  The 
recommended negative 90 percent estimate is also consistent with estimates for other 
companies in the industry. 
Another large plant account utilized to demonstrate the process to determine net 
salvage estimates for the depreciation study is Account 378, Measuring and Regulating 
Station Equipment.  The same range of company data from 1983 through 2019 was 
used for the historical analyses of this account.  The current estimate for the account is 
negative 20 percent net salvage.  While recent years have experienced limited cost of 
removal, this is not typical of the full data set and not considered to be indicative of 
future experience.  There are typically costs involved with replacing assets at regulator 
stations or retiring entire stations.  The overall net salvage from the data indicates a net 
salvage percent of negative 21 percent, which continues to support the current 
estimate.  The negative 20 percent net salvage estimate continues to be reasonable for 
this account. 
The net salvage estimates for the remaining plant accounts were estimated 
using the above-described process of historical indications, judgment and reviewing the 
typical ranges of estimates used by other gas companies.  The net salvage analyses 
for each plant account are presented in account sequence beginning in the section 
titled “Net Salvage Statistics”, page VIII-2.

PART V. CALCULATION OF ANNUAL AND 
ACCRUED DEPRECIATION 
 
GROUP DEPRECIATION PROCEDURES 
 
A group procedure for depreciation is appropriate when considering more than a 
single item of property. Normally the items within a group do not have identical service 
lives but have lives that are dispersed over a range of time. There are two primary group 
procedures, namely, average service life and equal life group. In the average service life 
procedure, the rate of annual depreciation is based on the average life or average 
remaining life of the group, and this rate is applied to the surviving balances of the 
group's cost.  A characteristic of this procedure is that the cost of plant retired prior to 
average life is not fully recouped at the time of retirement, whereas the cost of plant 
retired subsequent to average life is more than fully recouped. Over the entire life cycle, 
the portion of cost not recovered prior to average life is balanced by the cost recovered 
subsequent to average life. 
Single Unit of Property 
 
The calculation of straight-line depreciation for a single unit of property is 
straightforward. For example, if a $1,000 unit of property attains an age of four years and 
has a life expectancy of six years, the annual accrual over the total life is: 


$1,000
4 +  6
 $100 per year.
 
 
 
The accrued depreciation is: 
$1,000 1 -  6
10
 $400.

·
¹¸

Group Depreciation Procedures 
In the average service life procedure, the annual accrual rate is computed by the 
following equation:

ܣݒ݁ݎܽ݃݁ ܵ݁ݎݒ݅ܿ݁ ܮ݂݅݁
The calculated accrued depreciation for each depreciable property group 
represents that portion of the depreciable cost of the group which would not be 
allocated to expense through future depreciation accruals if current forecasts of life 
characteristics are used as the basis for such accruals. The accrued depreciation 
calculation consists of applying an appropriate ratio to the surviving original cost of each 
vintage of each account based upon the attained age and service life.  The straight line 
accrued depreciation ratios are calculated as follows for the average service life 
procedure: 
ܴܽݐ݅݋= 1 െ ܣݒ݁ݎܽ݃݁ ܴ݁݉ܽ݅݊݅݊݃ ܮ݂݅݁
ܣݒ݁ݎܽ݃݁ ܵ݁ݎݒ݅ܿ݁ ܮ݂݅݁
CALCULATION OF ANNUAL AND ACCRUED AMORTIZATION 
Amortization is the gradual extinguishment of an amount in an account by 
distributing such amount over a fixed period, over the life of the asset or liability to which 
it applies, or over the period during which it is anticipated the benefit will be realized. 
Normally, the distribution of the amount is in equal amounts to each year of the 
amortization period. 
The calculation of annual and accrued amortization requires the selection of an 
amortization period. The amortization periods used in this report were based on judgment 
which incorporated a consideration of the period during which the assets will provide 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
Orange & Rockland Utilities, Inc. 
December 31, 2019
V-3
Exhibit___DP-G1
50 of 192


--- Page 87 ---
 
most of their service, the amortization period and service lives used by other utilities, and 
the service life estimates previously used for the asset under depreciation accounting. 
Amortization accounting is currently used for several accounts that represent 
numerous units of property, but only a small portion of depreciable utility plant in service.  
No changes to the existing amortization periods are recommended in the study.   
MONITORING OF BOOK ACCUMULATED DEPRECIATION 
 
As stated previously, the calculated accrued depreciation or amortization represents that 
portion of the depreciable cost which will not be allocated to expense through future 
depreciation accruals, if current forecasts of service life characteristics and net salvage 
materialize and are used as a basis for depreciation accounting. Thus, the calculated 
accrued depreciation provides a measure of the book accumulated depreciation. The use 
of this measure is recommended in the adjustment of book accumulated depreciation 
variation to insure complete recovery of capital over the life of the property. The study 
has estimated reserve variations of negative $37,161,974 for gas plant and negative 
$1,226,189 for common plant as of December 31, 2019 based on the results of the 
updated service life and net salvage studies.  For this study, an adjustment is 
recommended to address the reserve variation for gas plant.  The reserve variation 
should be amortized over a twenty-year period, which has been a common period of 
addressing reserve variations in New York.  It is particularly important to address the 
reserve variation for gas plant due to the considerations discussed in Part III related to 
New York’s goals for decarbonization by the year 2050.  A twenty-year amortization will 
address the reserve variation estimated in this study prior to 2050.

PART VI.  RESULTS OF STUDY 
 
QUALIFICATION OF RESULTS 
 
The calculated annual and accrued depreciation are the principal results of the 
study. Continued surveillance and periodic revisions are normally required to maintain 
continued use of appropriate annual depreciation accrual rates. An assumption that 
accrual rates can remain unchanged over a long period of time implies a disregard for the 
inherent variability in service lives and net salvage and for the change of the composition 
of property in service. The annual accrual rates were calculated in accordance with the 
straight-line whole life method of depreciation, using the average service life procedure 
based on estimates which reflect considerations of current historical evidence and 
expected future conditions. 
The annual depreciation accrual rates are applicable specifically to the gas plant 
and common plant (at 100%) in service as of December 31, 2019. For most plant 
accounts, the application of such rates to future balances that reflect additions 
subsequent to December 31, 2019, is reasonable for a period of three to five years. 
 
DESCRIPTION OF DETAILED TABULATIONS 
 
Tables 1 presents a summary of the results of the study as applied to the original 
cost of gas and common plant as of December 31, 2019. The summary schedules for 
each business division are presented on pages VI-4 through VI-6 of this report. 
The service life estimates were based on informed judgment that incorporated 
statistical analysis of retirement data, discussions with management and consideration of 
estimates made for other gas utilities.  The results of the statistical analysis of service life 
are presented in the section beginning on page VII-2, within the supporting documents of

this report. 
For each depreciable group analyzed by the retirement rate method, a chart 
depicting the original and estimated survivor curves followed by a tabular presentation of 
the original life table(s) plotted on the chart. The survivor curves estimated for the 
depreciable groups are shown as dark smooth curves on the charts. Each smooth 
survivor curve is denoted by a numeral followed by the curve type designation. The 
numeral used is the average life derived from the entire curve from 100 percent to zero 
percent surviving. The titles of the chart indicate the group, the symbol used to plot the 
points of the original life table, and the experience and placement bands of the life tables 
which where plotted. The experience band indicates the range of years for which 
retirements were used to develop the stub survivor curve. The placements indicate, for 
the related experience band, the range of years of installations that appear in the 
experience. 
The analysis of net salvage data is presented in the section titled, “Net Salvage 
Statistics”. The tabulations present annual cost of removal and gross salvage data, three-
year moving averages and the most recent five-year average. Data are shown in dollars 
and as percentages of original costs retired. 
The tables of the calculated annual depreciation applicable to depreciable assets 
as of December 31, 2019 are presented in account sequence starting on page IX-3 of the 
supporting documents. The tables indicate the estimated survivor curve and net salvage 
percent for the account and set forth, for each installation year, the original cost, the 
average service life, the calculated annual rate and accrual, the remaining life, and the 
calculated accrued depreciation factor and amount.
